In 1972, Switzerland's total national income, including capital income, will be about 100 billion francs.
By about 1991, Switzerland will have twice as many AVS pensioners as in 1971, so that the ratio will be one pensioner for every two active people rather than one for every three.
When the eighth AVS revision enters into force, the relevant index will be one third higher than in December 1969.
In 1972, the Swiss Parliament will debate the two AVS reform proposals: the Parti du travail initiative and the Federal Council counterproject.
In 1972, the Swiss Parliament will recommend that the people reject the Parti du travail initiative and accept the Federal Council counterproject on AVS reform.
Swiss voters will be called to vote on the AVS reform question at the end of 1972, or at the latest in early 1973.
Even if a second referendum on the AVS proposals is rejected, the eighth AVS revision will take place in 1973.
The eighth AVS revision will be carried out and will enter into force on 1 January 1973.
The eighth AVS revision will be implemented in two stages, with the first stage entering into force in 1973.
In 1975, as the second stage of the eighth AVS revision, AVS pensions will be increased by a further 25%.
Existing AVS pensioners will receive the same benefit improvements as future pensioners under the eighth AVS revision, whose first stage enters into force in 1973.
In 1973, the combined AVS, disability insurance and loss-of-earnings contribution rate in Switzerland will rise from 6.2% to 8.4%.
In 1973, the employee's share of the AVS contribution in Switzerland will rise from 3.1% to 4.2%.
AVS expenditure in Switzerland will reach 6 billion francs in 1973.
In 1973, after the first stage of the eighth AVS revision, the annual contribution of the Confederation and cantons to AVS will rise from about 1.5 billion francs to 2 billion francs.
In 1978, five years after the first post-revision increase, the annual contribution of the Confederation and cantons to AVS will reach 2.5 billion francs.
From 1973 to 1977, public authorities in Switzerland will continue to pay one fifth of AVS expenditure, and from 1978 they will pay one quarter.
In 1973, the additional 500 million francs in AVS costs from the first stage of the eighth revision will likely be financed by higher taxes on tobacco and cigarettes.
After the eighth AVS revision is fully implemented in 1975, the simple old-age pension in Switzerland will range from 400 to 800 francs per month.
After the eighth AVS revision is fully implemented in 1975, married couples in Switzerland will receive a 50% supplement and a monthly pension ranging from 600 to 1,200 francs.
After the eighth AVS revision is fully implemented in 1975, AVS contributions in Switzerland will have no ceiling.

The parties to the EEA Agreement will carry out the first biennial review of their trade in processed agricultural products before the end of 1993.
Under the EEA protocol on processed agricultural products, the parties will reduce customs duties and other fixed amounts on the products listed in Article 2 of Appendix 1, including HS 2007 jams and marmalades, HS 1302 pectic substances with added sugar of at least 5%, HS 1517 margarine with 10-15% milkfat, and HS 2106 food preparations with more than 15% milkfat, to five-sixths of their base level on 1 January 1993 and will make five further one-sixth reductions on 1 January 1994, 1 January 1995, 1 January 1996, 1 January 1997 and 1 January 1998.
Under the EEA protocol on processed agricultural products, the parties will reduce customs duties and other fixed amounts on HS heading 1702 chemically pure fructose to 90% of their base level on 1 January 1993 and will make four further 10% reductions on 1 January 1994, 1 January 1995, 1 January 1996 and 1 January 1997.
Under the EEA protocol on processed agricultural products, the parties will reduce customs duties and other fixed amounts on HS heading 1302 pectic substances, pectinates and pectates with added sugar content below 5% by weight to five-sixths of their base level on 1 January 1993 and will make five further one-sixth reductions on 1 January 1994, 1 January 1995, 1 January 1996, 1 January 1997 and 1 January 1998.
Under the EEA protocol on processed agricultural products, the parties will reduce customs duties and other fixed amounts on HS heading 1702 chemically pure maltose to 90% of their base level on 1 January 1993 and will make four further 10% reductions on 1 January 1994, 1 January 1995, 1 January 1996 and 1 January 1997.
The temporary regime for Iceland's processed agricultural products will be reviewed by the parties to the EEA Agreement before the end of 1998.
Austria will apply Article 16 of the EEA Agreement to products under HS heading 2208 by 1 January 1996 at the latest.
Austria will liberalise its licensing regime for products under HS heading 2208 and grant licences automatically from 1 January 1993.
Austria will progressively eliminate customs duties on spirits and undenatured ethyl alcohol of less than 80% vol under HS heading 2208 between 1 January 1993 and 1 January 1996.
Austria will abolish import duties from 1 January 1993 on Irish whiskey, rum and tafia, Irish cream and ouzo under HS heading 2208.
Austria will apply the EEA Agreement to the listed products under HS headings 3505, 3809 and 3823, including modified starches, adhesives, textile and paper finishing agents, and foundry binders, by 1 January 1997 at the latest.

The new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968 will replace the social insurance convention of 16 January 1953 and the supplementary convention of 12 November 1959.
Under the new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968, British nationals will be entitled to ordinary Swiss old-age, survivors' and invalidity insurance pensions after one full year of contributions.
Under the new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968, British nationals residing in Switzerland will be eligible for extraordinary Swiss old-age pensions on the same conditions as Swiss nationals if they can show at least 10 years of domicile in Switzerland at the time of applying for the pension.
Under the new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968, British nationals residing in Switzerland will be eligible for extraordinary Swiss survivors' and invalidity pensions, or old-age pensions replacing them, on the same conditions as Swiss nationals if they can show at least 5 years of domicile in Switzerland at the time of applying for the pension.
Under the new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968, rehabilitation measures will be granted to British nationals if they are domiciled in Switzerland and have paid contributions to the Swiss pension insurance for at least one full year.
Under the new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968, the United Kingdom will aggregate Swiss and UK insurance periods for entitlement to British old-age and death benefits whenever a Swiss or British national has completed at least one year of insurance in the UK and one year in Switzerland and does not meet the UK entitlement conditions on UK periods alone.
Under the new Switzerland–United Kingdom social security convention signed on 21 February 1968, British family allowances will be paid for children residing in Switzerland.

The new Swiss federal framework credit of 680 million Swiss francs for Switzerland's participation in the capital increases of the Inter-American Development Bank, the Asian Development Bank and the African Development Bank and for accession to the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency will have a minimum term of four years and will enter into force no earlier than 1 July 1987.
Disbursements under the new Swiss federal framework credit of 680 million Swiss francs for Switzerland's participation in the capital increases of the Inter-American Development Bank, the Asian Development Bank and the African Development Bank and for accession to the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency will, from 1987 onwards, be spread over about twelve years and will be submitted to Parliament as payment credits within the annual budget.
As of January 1987, private financial flows to developing countries will not increase again in the short term.
In the years after 1987, repayments of loans granted before 1980 to the ordinary capital and development funds of the Inter-American Development Bank, the Asian Development Bank and the African Development Bank will become an increasingly important source for granting new loans.
In the seventh replenishment period (1987-1990) of the Inter-American Development Bank's Fund for Special Operations, a lending programme of 1,844 million US dollars could be financed with the same amount of new member contributions as in the sixth replenishment period, namely 703 million US dollars.
In the years following 1987, the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will use about 20% of its loan volume for current import programmes, initially in agriculture, industry and energy supply.
As of January 1987, the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will make its financing decision conditional on the recipient government's agreement to a specific reform package for the relevant economic sector.
As of January 1987, IDB loan disbursements will be divided into two or more tranches staggered over time, and later tranches will be preceded by a joint IDB and recipient-country assessment of progress in implementing the agreed reform steps.
As of January 1987, in some cases, IDB sector credits will be cofinanced with the World Bank.
As of January 1987, during loan preparation, the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will support the member country through studies and technical staff in planning economic-policy reforms.
In the years following 1987, about 10% of the African Development Bank's (BAD) loan volume will be allocated to programme credits, mainly in the form of cofinancing with the International Development Association (IDA).
As of January 1987, the Directorate of the African Development Bank (BAD) will strengthen its technical staff and call in experts from other institutions.
As of January 1987, in Pakistan, the Asian Development Bank (ADB) will promote small enterprises and especially the processing of agricultural products, through loans via local industrial banks, direct ADB equity participations and the establishment of industrial zones.
The Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA) will be founded in the second half of 1987, after at least ten developing countries and five industrial countries representing at least one third of MIGA's initial capital of one billion special drawing rights have ratified the MIGA convention.
Switzerland will hold the post of deputy executive director at the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) from 1988 to 1991.
Switzerland will hold the post of deputy executive director at the Asian Development Bank (ADB) for the term running from 1986 to 1989.
Switzerland will provide a technical assistant to the executive director of the Asian Development Bank (ADB) from 1990 to 1992.
Switzerland will hold the post of deputy executive director at the African Development Bank (BAD) for the term running from 1986 to 1990.
Switzerland will hold the post of executive director at the African Development Bank (BAD) from 1992 to 1994.
As of January 1987, before starting operations in a recipient country, the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA) will conclude an agreement with that country on dispute settlement procedures.
As of January 1987, in the medium term, the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA) will be able to insure investments worth about 10 billion Swiss francs.
As of January 1987, the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA) will conclude agreements with state guarantee agencies on cooperation in issuing guarantees.
Cooperation agreements between the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA) and state guarantee agencies will lead to additional guarantees for Swiss investors at more favourable conditions than in 1987, and will make the activities of the Swiss Investment Risk Guarantee (IRG) more beneficial.
As of January 1987, Switzerland will use the remaining 15.7 million Swiss francs of the previous framework credit for future obligations towards the regional development banks.
If the 680 million Swiss franc framework credit is approved, Switzerland will be able to maintain its membership in the regional development banks at the previous capital share for at least four years from 1 July 1987, and to finance its accession to the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA).
The capital subscriptions for the seventh capital increase of the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) and the fourth capital increase of the Asian Development Bank (ADB) will become due in the course of 1987.
As of January 1987, Switzerland's previous capital share in the three regional development banks (IDB, ADB and AfDB) will in principle be maintained.
As of January 1987, individual development banks may adjust their capital structure, which could lead Switzerland to negotiate changes in its share in order to protect Swiss interests.
As of January 1987, if the Federal Assembly rejects Switzerland's accession to the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA), the framework credit will be reduced by the capital share of 33 million Swiss francs necessary for membership.
As part of its seventh capital increase for 1987-1990, the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will introduce sector adjustment loans amounting to 20% of its lending.
A fourth capital increase of the Asian Development Bank (ADB) will not be necessary before 1989.
A proposal for the next capital increase of the Asian Development Bank (ADB) will be submitted to its Board of Directors by the end of 1987.
As of January 1987, bottlenecks in the availability of external financing for the Asian region are expected.
As of January 1987, demand for Asian Development Bank (ADB) loans will continue to increase.
From 1986 onwards, India will regularly borrow from the Asian Development Bank (ADB).
As of January 1987, the African Development Bank (BAD) will carry out a substantial capital increase.
The African Development Bank (BAD)'s lending volume for 1987 to 1991 will amount to approximately 7.3 billion US dollars, corresponding to an annual real growth of 8.7%.
Of the African Development Bank (BAD)'s lending for 1987-1991, one quarter will go to Nigeria and Algeria, 31% to three other North African states, and the remaining 44% to 13 sub-Saharan countries.
As of January 1987, Switzerland will be assigned a capital share of 15 million SDR (about 33 million Swiss francs) in the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA), of which 3.3 million francs (10%) will be paid within three months of accession.
The new 680 million Swiss franc framework credit will not be drawn before the existing credit is fully exhausted, and not before 1 July 1987.
Payments under the new framework credit will extend over about twelve years beginning no earlier than 1 July 1987 and will be submitted for approval in the annual budget.

As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, the expert costs of a special audit will in most cases exceed 30,000 Swiss francs.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, the pressure of the public and the financial and trade press will be sufficient to compel companies that meet the conditions for disclosure to publish their annual and consolidated accounts, so that no special control mechanism is needed.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, in many cases the general meeting of a Swiss company will not approve a shareholder's request to appoint a special auditor.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, normally a shareholder who requests a special audit at the general meeting of a large Swiss company will not have the required minority (10% of share capital or shares with a nominal value of 1 million francs) to force the appointment of a special auditor.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, the criterion of holding at least one tenth of the share capital will likely help minority shareholders of small and medium-sized Swiss companies to effectively defend themselves against arbitrary rule by majority shareholders.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, the main case of a special audit will be the investigation of whether there has been a violation of law or statutes by the management, the board of directors, or the auditors.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, a special auditor appointed will take care not to unnecessarily disclose business secrets.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, in a dispute over a shareholder's right to information, the judge will have to hear the company's objection that the necessary information has already been provided to the shareholder.
As of July 1983, under the proposed revision of Swiss company law, in deciding on a shareholder's right to inspect the company's books, the judge will in particular examine whether the right of inspection can be satisfied by the presentation of certified extracts.
As of July 1983, the revised Swiss company law will extend the minimum notice period for general meetings from 10 to 20 days.
As of July 1983, the revised Swiss company law will allow shareholders representing shares with a nominal value of 1 million francs to request the convening of a general meeting.
The expansion of the right to convene a general meeting will be relevant for Swiss companies with share capital over 10 million francs (751 companies, just under 0.7% of all stock corporations according to 1980 statistics).
As of July 1983, under the revised Swiss company law, a violation of the ten-board-mandate limit for listed companies will make the election of the board member contestable under Article 706.
As of July 1983, the new qualified majority requirement for important shareholder decisions will weaken but not abolish the influence of voting-right shares, unlike Article 693 para 3 of the current Code of Obligations.
As of July 1983, giving the board president a casting vote will exclude deadlocks in the board of directors and ensure decision-making for daily business that cannot be delayed.
As of July 1983, the limit of ten board mandates will apply only to companies whose shares are listed on a stock exchange or traded over-the-counter.
As of July 1983, the revised Swiss company law will allow a departing board member to apply to the commercial register for their own removal after 30 days.
As of July 1983, the revised Swiss company law will be brought into force by the Swiss Federal Council at the beginning of the second half of the year (1 July), not on 1 January.
As of July 1983, under the revised Swiss company law, the obligation to prepare consolidated accounts (Konzernrechnung, Article 663d) will enter into force after the other provisions.
As of July 1983, under the revised Swiss company law, the professional requirements for auditors will enter into force after the other provisions.
As of July 1983, the draft revision of the Swiss stock corporation law will increase the workload of the Swiss commercial register offices by about 10% on average nationwide.
As of July 1983, the additional staffing required by the draft revision of the Swiss stock corporation law will not lead to an additional financial burden for the Swiss cantons and the Confederation.
Neuchâtel Xamax will win the Swiss football championship in the 1985-86 season.
Neuchâtel Xamax will resume official competition with a match against Servette on 28 February 1986.
Neuchâtel Xamax will play the first leg of its UEFA Cup quarter-final on 5 March 1986.
The National League and the Swiss Football Association (ASF) will decide in spring 1986 on the proposed Swiss football centre in the Littoral region.
If Neuchâtel is selected as one of the six Swiss host cities for the 1992 European Football Championship, the seating capacity of Stade de la Maladière in Neuchâtel will be increased from 4,000 to 5,000.
The introduction of the 5.6-mm Sturmgewehr 90 into the Swiss Army will be completed only in the second half of the 1990s, between 1995 and 1999.
A second large lot of 5.6-mm Sturmgewehr 90 rifles will be requested for procurement in the 1990 Swiss armaments programme.
The remaining 13,000 Sturmgewehr 90 rifles from the 1983 Swiss armaments programme will be delivered in 1988 and 1989.
The conversion of Swiss Army units to the 5.6-mm Sturmgewehr 90 will begin in 1988 with the issue of rifles from the 1983 armaments programme.
From 1988, rifle ammunition for the Swiss Army will be manufactured only at the Federal Munitions Factory in Thun, not at Altdorf.
The ignition elements for the 5.6-mm Gewehrpatrone 90, previously bought abroad, will be manufactured in Switzerland under licence (plan as of April 1987).
A decision on whether to introduce the newly developed propellant powder for the 5.6-mm Gewehrpatrone 90 may be taken by the end of 1988 at the latest.
As of April 1987, construction of the production facility at the Federal Powder Factory Wimmis for the new propellant powder will be postponed, except for the already approved storage buildings.
Any investments for the later production in Switzerland of propellant powder for the 5.6-mm Sturmgewehr 90 will be presented in the 1990 armaments programme together with the procurement of the second large lot of Sturmgewehr 90 rifles.
Smoke and fragmentation shells for the 6-cm Werfer 87 will be requested for procurement in the early 1990s.
As of April 1987, the guided-missile and cannon anti-tank formations of the Swiss Army will be the first units equipped with the 6-cm Werfer 87.
As of April 1987, the 6-cm Werfer 87 will be assigned to the infantry, mechanized troops and light troops of the Swiss Army.
The 6-cm mortars (6-cm-Werfer) will be delivered to the Swiss Army between 1990 and 1993.
The 6-cm illumination shells (Beleuchtungsgeschosse) will be delivered to the Swiss Army between 1990 and 1994.
The 12-cm Minenwerfer 74 will be delivered to the Swiss Army, with the pilot lot in 1989 and the series from 1990 to 1993.
The ammunition for the 12-cm Minenwerfer 74 (including high-explosive, illumination, and practice rounds) will be delivered between 1989 and 1993.
27 new Swiss Army heavy mortar companies (Schwere Minenwerferkompanien) will be formed between 1991 and 1993, simultaneously with the introduction of the tank destroyer (Panzerjäger).
As of April 1987, all existing and new Swiss Army heavy mortar companies will be uniformly equipped with the Laser-Goniometer 85.
As of April 1987, nine Swiss Army anti-tank guided missile companies equipped with the Bantam missile (Auszug/Landwehr) will be dissolved.
As of April 1987, annual maintenance costs for the 6-cm mortar system will be around 500,000 Swiss francs.
As of April 1987, annual costs for training ammunition for the 6-cm mortar system will be around 10 million Swiss francs.
The laser rangefinder material requested for the Swiss Army will be delivered in 1989-1990.
The follow-on procurement of laser rangefinder material will provide average employment for 28 people over about three years, from 1989 to about 1992.
As of April 1987, the creation of 27 additional heavy mortar companies will cause an estimated additional annual expenditure of 10.4 million Swiss francs.
As of April 1987, the buildings needed for storage of the additional heavy mortar equipment will cost about 11 million Swiss francs.
As of April 1987, funding for the additional storage buildings will be requested mainly through projects for underground ammunition facilities in future Swiss construction messages.
Replacing the material taken from reserves will cost about 36 million Swiss francs in the budgets for personal equipment and renewal following April 1987.
The first-generation 10.5 cm arrow ammunition introduced in the Swiss Army will soon, after April 1987, no longer be sufficient against the newest tank armour.
As of April 1987, the new second-generation 10.5 cm arrow ammunition will be partly produced under licence at the Swiss Federal Munitions Factory in Altdorf.
As of April 1987, the second-generation 10.5 cm arrow ammunition will be assigned to the Swiss Army's basic and supplementary ammunition stocks and reserves.
Qualification firings to evaluate the Swiss-produced propellant powder for the new arrow ammunition will be carried out in 1987.
As of April 1987, if the Swiss-produced propellant powder fails the licensor's qualification, the powder will be procured abroad and the Swiss share of the procurement will fall by 13 percentage points to 42 percent.
The first tranche of second-generation 10.5 cm arrow ammunition will be purchased from the licensor in 1988.
The radar warning system material will be delivered to the Swiss Federal Aircraft Factory Emmen from 1989 to 1991.
The final software for the new radar warning system, due in March 1987, will subsequently be tested and flight-tested.
As of April 1987, the manufacturer of the radar warning system will place direct and indirect orders with Swiss industry worth about 65 million Swiss francs, about 60 percent of the contract volume.
The Swiss Mirage fleet will be equipped with the new radar warning system by the end of 1991.
The entire Swiss Tiger fleet will be retrofitted with the new radar warning system by mid-1992.
The Vampire jet aircraft used for Swiss military pilot training will be withdrawn from service by 1990 at the latest.
The new Hawk jet trainer will remain in service with the Swiss Air Force for at least 25 years after its introduction, i.e. until at least 2015.
Delivery of the Hawk jet trainers to the Swiss Air Force will begin in the first quarter of 1990 and last about one year.
The first Swiss pilot school using the Hawk jet trainer will be held in summer 1990, with the flight simulator put into operation at the same time.
An initial team of Swiss flight instructors will be trained on the Hawk in a pilot school in the manufacturer's country in spring 1990.
The new Swiss military pilot training course will be held for the first time in 1987, and both pilot schools will then be conducted twice a year.
The United States armed forces will procure more than 300 Hawk aircraft in the years after 1987.
The first Hawk jet trainer aircraft will be delivered by British Aerospace to Switzerland at the end of 1989.
The 19 Hawk jet trainer aircraft assembled in Switzerland will be delivered to the Swiss Armed Forces between early 1990 and early 1991.
The Hawk flight simulator will be available to the Swiss Armed Forces in early summer 1990.
Delivery of all remaining Hawk procurement material will be completed by the end of 1992.
British Aerospace will have fulfilled its economic offset obligation to Swiss industry by the end of 1993.
The UHF ground radio equipment for the Swiss Air Force will be delivered in 1989 and 1990.
As of April 1987, Swiss industry will have a share of about 10% by value in the electronics portion of the UHF ground radio procurement for the Swiss Air Force.
Swiss industry will receive indirect participation worth 20% of the CHF 26 million foreign UHF ground radio material within five years, by 1992.
As of April 1987, J. Sauter AG will pass 60% of the mobile sterilization trailer order to subcontractors.
As of April 1987, 53% of the supply volume for the mobile sterilization power generator will be awarded to subcontractors in Switzerland.
As of April 1987, the mobile sterilization trailer order will be 100% effective in terms of employment in Switzerland.
As of April 1987, direct participation in the Hawk procurement will bring the Swiss aviation industry orders worth about CHF 60 million, equivalent to about 210 man-years of employment.
As of April 1987, indirect participation in the Hawk procurement will bring Swiss industry additional orders worth about CHF 40 million.
As of April 1987, eight private firms in German-speaking Switzerland and four in western Switzerland will likely be directly involved in producing the Hawk aircraft.
As of April 1987, the total labor effort for preparing and maintaining the Hawk fleet per flight hour will be about half that for the Vampire aircraft.
As of April 1987, each Hawk jet trainer will consume about 40% less fuel than a Vampire aircraft in flight operations.
As of April 1987, the total cost per flight hour for the Hawk jet trainer will be about 40% lower than for the Vampire.
As of April 1987, once 63 Vampire and 34 Vampire trainers are withdrawn from service, the 20 Hawk jet trainers, including personnel costs, will produce annual savings of about CHF 7 million.
As of April 1987, the 20 new Hawk jet trainers will fly the same number of flight hours annually as the 97 Vampire aircraft.

The proposed amendments to the Swiss Civil Servants Act will enter into force on 1 January 1989.
The Swiss federal civil service salary scale will include six additional salary classes between the former class 5 and level VII of the overclass under the amendment entering into force on 1 January 1989.
On 1 January 1989, the maximum amounts of the Swiss federal civil service salary scale will be raised by a real 2%.
On 1 January 1989, the seven overclass levels of the Swiss federal civil service salary scale will be raised by 2%.
The entitlement of unmarried, widowed and divorced Swiss federal employees to the higher local allowance will shift from household dependency to a maintenance or support obligation under the amendment entering into force on 1 January 1989.
Swiss federal employers will be allowed to pay a special allowance of up to CHF 2,000 per year to all or certain categories of staff in locations where recruiting and retaining personnel is extraordinarily difficult, under the amendment entering into force on 1 January 1989.
Swiss federal personnel will receive an autumn allowance in 1988.
The Swiss federal decree on cost-of-living allowances for federal personnel will be extended for the years 1989–1992.
Federal employees not covered by the Swiss Civil Servants Act will receive the same improvements as civil servants under the proposed measures entering into force in 1989.
After 1991, the additional annual cost of all proposed measures for Swiss federal personnel salaries will be about CHF 390 million.
Switzerland's real gross national product will grow by 1.7% to 1.8% in 1987.
Switzerland's real gross domestic product will rise by more than 2% per year in 1987.
Switzerland's domestic final demand will grow at rates of 2.5% to over 4% up to 1988.
Switzerland's production will grow by around 2% in 1988, with a somewhat weaker economic situation.
The number of young people in Switzerland entering working life after leaving school will decline markedly until well into the 1990s.
The number of jobs in Switzerland's service sector will continue to rise after 1987.
The Swiss Federal Railways will face rising replacement staffing needs in the next few years after 1987.
In the major Swiss agglomerations of Geneva, Zurich, Basel, Lausanne and partly Bern, recruiting enough well-trained staff and apprentices for monopoly professions will become increasingly difficult in the years after 1987.
As of December 1987, entitlement to the higher location allowance for married employees for single, widowed and divorced Swiss federal employees will newly depend on the existence of a legal maintenance or support obligation.
As of December 1987, for locations with exceptional difficulties in recruiting or retaining Swiss federal personnel, an additional location allowance of up to 2,000 francs per year may be granted.
As of December 1987, six new salary classes will be inserted between the current 5th salary class and top class level VII in the Swiss federal salary scale.
As of December 1987, the maximum of the 4th salary class in the Swiss federal salary scale will be raised by 100 francs.
As of December 1987, raising the maximum of the 4th salary class by 100 francs will cost about 300,000 francs in steady state.
As of December 1987, from the 12th salary class upward, the difference between minimum and maximum salary amounts in the Swiss federal salary scale will be made equal in francs.
As of December 1987, corrections to minimum salary amounts in individual classes of the Swiss federal salary scale will amount to at most ±600 francs per year.
At the end of 1987, Swiss federal salaries will lag private-sector wages by about 5 percent.
After 1991, the targeted salary measures for Swiss federal personnel will cause annual additional costs of about 3 percent.
A linear real wage increase of 2 percent for Swiss federal personnel will be granted on 1 January 1989.
The real wage backlog of Swiss federal personnel will be compensated at the earliest from the end of 1991.
The 2 percent real wage increase will take effect from 1989 only on maximum salary amounts; minimum salary amounts will remain unchanged.
On 1 January 1989, the Swiss Federal Council intends to raise the salaries of all officials in the top class by 2 percent.
As of December 1987, the maximum salary amount for the top class in the Swiss federal salary scale will be increased by 5 percent.
As of December 1987, the Swiss federal salary scale will be renumbered so that the former lowest 24th class becomes class 1 and the highest class becomes class 31.
As of December 1987, a new paragraph will be added to Article 37 of the Swiss Civil Servants Act to provide a legal basis for location-allowance special cases.
A one-time, non-insurable fixed autumn allowance of 600 francs will be paid to all Swiss federal employees in October 1988, regardless of salary class.
As of December 1987, the existing rules on inflation compensation for Swiss federal personnel will be extended by four years.
The revision of the job classification for Swiss federal officials will be implemented in stages over four personnel budget periods from 1 July 1988 to the end of 1991.
The salary-scale, location-allowance and job-classification improvements, the 1988 autumn allowance and the extension of inflation compensation will also be granted to other employees in a direct employment relationship with the Swiss Confederation.
On 1 January 1989, only the maximum amounts of the Swiss federal salary classes will be raised by 2%.
On 1 January 1989, the Swiss federal salary scale under Article 36 of the Civil Servants Act will be expanded from 25 to 31 classes by inserting six additional classes.
The renumbering of the Swiss federal salary scale on 1 January 1989 will not cause any additional costs.
As of December 1987, the revision of the Swiss federal office classification will be implemented in stages over about three and a half years rather than on a single date.
After the new law enters into force on 1 January 1989, for Swiss federal civil servants who received the higher married person's local allowance under the old law but cannot claim it under the new law, the existing difference between the two allowances will be paid in full for five years and at half for a further five years, and the guarantee will expire after ten years.
A one-time, non-insurable autumn allowance of CHF 600 will be paid to Swiss federal civil servants together with their October 1988 salary.
Swiss federal employees who enter the federal service after 31 October 1988 or whose employment has been terminated on 1 October 1988 will not receive the 1988 autumn allowance.
For Swiss federal employees who join or leave during 1988, the 1988 autumn allowance will be reduced in proportion to their service time, and part-time employees will receive it according to their employment level.
The Swiss federal resolution on inflation allowances for federal personnel will be extended until 31 December 1992.
The amended Swiss Civil Servants Act (Beamtengesetz) will enter into force on 1 January 1989.
The amended Swiss federal resolution on inflation allowances for federal personnel will enter into force on 1 January 1989.
The Swiss federal resolution on the 1988 autumn allowance for federal personnel will enter into force on 1 October 1988.
Once fully implemented, the revision of the Swiss federal office classification, the other targeted measures and the 2% real salary increase will cause additional personnel costs of about CHF 390 million, or 5% of personnel expenditure under the 1987 budget.
By 1991, including employer contributions to AHV/IV/EO/ALV and SUVA at about 7%, the total additional costs of the measures will be about CHF 420 million.
As of December 1987, at the Swiss Federal Railways (SBB), the increased personnel costs and employer pension contributions will worsen the financial results and reduce the contribution to federal infrastructure expenditure.
As of December 1987, in the PTT accounts, the increased personnel costs and employer pension contributions will reduce the company's profit.
As of December 1987, the measures will increase recurring pension contributions for the general Swiss federal administration by CHF 10 million.
As of December 1987, the measures will increase recurring pension contributions for the PTT by CHF 11.8 million.
As of December 1987, the measures will increase recurring pension contributions for the Swiss Federal Railways (SBB) by CHF 7.5 million.
As of December 1987, the proposed Swiss federal personnel measures will have no direct financial consequences for the cantons and communes.
As of December 1987, the proposed amendments to the Civil Servants Act and the extension of the inflation allowance resolution will not increase staffing levels in the federal administration, cantons or communes.
Once it enters into force, the new Customs Convention on the International Transport of Goods under Cover of TIR Carnets, concluded on 14 November 1975, will repeal and replace the 1959 TIR Convention in relations between its contracting parties.
For Switzerland, application of the new TIR Convention of 14 November 1975 will have no financial repercussions and no effect on the number of staff.
As of April 1977, the Management Committee of the new TIR Convention will continue to meet under the chairmanship of the United Nations Economic Commission for Europe in Geneva.
On Monday 1 June 1981, the weather in the Geneva region and north of the Alps will be unstable and largely sunny, with a stronger tendency to thunderstorms in the evening, especially in mountainous areas.
In the Geneva region on Monday 1 June 1981, the temperature will be around 12°C at the end of the night and between 23°C and 28°C in the afternoon.
On Monday 1 June 1981, in the Geneva region and the northern Alps, the zero-degree isotherm will be at about 3,800 m and mountain winds will be moderate from the southwest.
On Tuesday 2 and Wednesday 3 June 1981, the weather in the Geneva region and north of the Alps will change little compared with Monday 1 June 1981.
The GATT protocol inserting Part IV on trade and development, signed on 8 February 1965, will enter into force once it has been accepted by two-thirds of the GATT's full member countries; for provisional members such as Switzerland, it will enter into force only after signature by two-thirds of the full members and acceptance by Switzerland.
The period for accepting the GATT Part IV protocol will provisionally expire on 31 December 1965.
The Swiss Federal Assembly will decide during its December 1965 session on the approval of the GATT protocol inserting Part IV on trade and development.
The declaration concerning Switzerland's provisional accession to the GATT will expire at the end of 1967.
As of September 1965, the GATT Part IV protocol will also apply to the Principality of Liechtenstein as long as Liechtenstein is linked to Switzerland by a customs union.
The protocol amending Part IV of the GATT, done at Geneva on 8 February 1965, will remain open for acceptance by GATT contracting parties and by governments that have acceded provisionally to the GATT until 31 December 1965.
The amendments to the GATT set out in paragraph 1 of the Protocol of 8 February 1965 will enter into force, in accordance with Article XXX of the GATT, once the Protocol has been accepted by two-thirds of the governments that are then GATT contracting parties.
The amendments to the GATT set out in paragraph 1 of the Protocol of 8 February 1965 will take effect between a government with provisional accession to the GATT and a GATT contracting party, or between two governments with provisional accession, once both have accepted the amendments; they will not take effect before a provisional accession instrument has taken effect between the two governments and before the amendments have taken effect under paragraph 4 of the Protocol.
Acceptance of the Protocol of 8 February 1965 by a GATT contracting party will constitute the final formality for that party to become party to each of the listed GATT amendment and rectification instruments, unless at the time of acceptance it sends the Executive Secretary a written notification to the contrary.
The Executive Secretary of the GATT contracting parties will promptly send a certified copy of the Protocol of 8 February 1965 to each GATT contracting party and each government with provisional accession to the GATT, and will promptly notify them of each acceptance of the Protocol.
The Protocol of 8 February 1965 will be registered in accordance with Article 102 of the UN Charter.
The winner of the 1972 Tour de Suisse will be decided on the final day of the race, in the individual time trial at Olten.
The 1973 5.5-metre class world championship will be held in Sweden in late July or early August 1973.
The 1974 5.5-metre class world championship will be held in Sydney, Australia, in January 1974.
The Swiss Federal Council's message (Botschaft) on the Chemicals Act (Chemikaliengesetz) will be issued in 1998.
The consultation on a draft Swiss Genetic Analysis Act (Genomanalysegesetz) will be opened in 1998.
Introducing a licensing requirement for xenotransplantations in Switzerland will require an amendment to the Federal Decree of 22 March 1996 on the control of blood, blood products and transplants, and therefore a separate message from the Federal Council to the Federal Assembly.
As of April 1998, the Swiss Federal Council will not pursue a separate coordination law on non-human genetic engineering.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex legislative package will create a Federal Ethics Commission for Non-Human Genetic Engineering in Switzerland.
As of April 1998, the planned Swiss Federal Ethics Commission for Non-Human Genetic Engineering will include representatives from ethics, natural sciences, medicine, law and economics.
As of April 1998, under the Gen-Lex amendments, mixtures and objects that may contain genetically modified organisms will have to be labelled in accordance with the relevant EU directive.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will introduce a public-access principle for information on the handling of organisms under the Swiss Environmental Protection Act.
As of April 1998, under the planned public-access principle, application documents will not be accessible while a permit application is pending or if they contain confidential production data.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will introduce a general strict liability rule in the Swiss Environmental Protection Act for facilities that pose a particular danger because they handle genetically modified or pathogenic organisms.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex package will extend the scope of the Swiss Product Liability Act to genetically modified agricultural products.
As of April 1998, if the EU changes its Product Liability Directive to cover unprocessed agricultural products, pressure will arise on Swiss legislation to delete Article 3(2) of the Swiss Product Liability Act in order not to endanger exports of agricultural products.
The Swiss Federal Council's message on the total revision of the Swiss Animal Protection Act will not be available before 1999.
As of April 1998, under the Gen-Lex amendments, the production, breeding, keeping and use of genetically modified animals in Switzerland will require a permit.
As of April 1998, the new Article 1b of the Swiss Animal Protection Act will subject the creation, breeding, keeping and use of transgenic animals to the same rules as animal experiments.
As of April 1998, if it is established that creating, breeding, keeping or using transgenic animals causes no pain, suffering, damage or behavioural disorders, the Swiss Federal Council will be allowed to grant exemptions from the permit requirement or simplify the procedure.
As of April 1998, the Swiss Federal Veterinary Office will be tasked with collecting information on genetic modifications of animals and informing the public about animal experiments and genetic modifications of animals.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will introduce the possibility of a labelling requirement for utility objects under the Swiss Food Act.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will give the Swiss Federal Council the power to introduce the same public-access principle in Swiss food law.
As of April 1998, the Swiss Federal Council will be authorized under the Swiss Epidemics Act to require labelling of objects that contain pathogens or are derived from pathogens.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will require labelling of immunological products that consist of or contain genetically modified organisms under the Swiss Epidemics Act.
As of April 1998, under new Article 24a of the Swiss Agriculture Act, genetically modified agricultural products or auxiliary substances may not be produced, bred, imported, released or placed on the market if they endanger human and environmental safety, violate the dignity of creatures, or impair biological diversity and its sustainable use.
As of April 1998, under new Article 24b of the Swiss Agriculture Act, labelling will be mandatory for agricultural products and their processed products and for auxiliary substances that consist of or contain genetically modified organisms.
As of April 1998, under new Article 144a of the Swiss Agriculture Act, breeding and import of genetically modified farm animals will be permitted only if important reasons for breeding and marketing exist and the requirements of the Environmental Protection Act and the Animal Protection Act are met.
As of April 1998, the Swiss Federal Council will be authorized to subject the import and placing on the market of agricultural auxiliary substances that consist of or contain genetically modified organisms to an approval requirement.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will introduce a labelling requirement in Article 27 of the Swiss Animal Diseases Act for immunological products and other preparations that consist of or contain genetically modified organisms.
As of April 1998, the Gen-Lex amendments will give the Swiss Federal Council the power to regulate labelling of transplants consisting of genetically modified organisms under the Federal Decree on the Control of Blood, Blood Products and Transplants.
As of April 1998, the labelling provision for transplants may later be transferred into a future Swiss federal regulation on xenotransplantation.
The amendments to Swiss law contained in the Gen-Lex package will not enter into force before 2000.
The Ethics Commission for non-human genetic engineering in Switzerland could meet for the first time in 2001.
The Swiss Federal Department of Economic Affairs (EVD) will present a draft federal ordinance on the Ethics Commission for non-human genetic engineering in the months following April 1998.
As of April 1998, if the number of applications for permits for genetically modified organisms increases, the Swiss federal offices handling them—the Federal Office for Environment, Forests and Landscape and the Federal Office of Public Health—will need to be expanded.

The statistics for the first half of 1972 on acquisitions of Swiss real estate by persons abroad will be worse than the 1971 figures.
The Swiss Federal Decree of 23 March 1961 on the authorization requirement for the acquisition of real estate by persons abroad will be extended by five years from the entry into force of the planned revision, and that entry into force will be coordinated with the repeal of the Federal Council's ordinance of 26 June 1972 prohibiting the investment of foreign funds in Swiss real estate.
As of November 1972, the Swiss Federal Council will tighten Article 4 of its ordinance implementing the authorization requirement for real-estate acquisitions by persons abroad, raising the requirements for legal persons to prove that their acquisition is not subject to authorization.
If the draft law amending the Federal Decree on the Acquisition of Real Estate by Foreigners is enacted as proposed in November 1972, the number of foreigners not subject to approval because of residence in Switzerland will decrease by about half, from approximately 484,000 to approximately 242,000.

As of October 1975, for any signatory state that ratifies, accepts or approves the convention signed in The Hague on 2 October 1973 after its entry into force, the convention will enter into force on the first day of the third calendar month after that state deposits its instrument of ratification, acceptance or approval.

The proposed changes to the federal decree on domestic sugar economy will come into force on 1 October of a sugar year (after 1984) and will remain in force until 30 September 1989.
From 1986, savings of up to 20 million Swiss francs are expected from the revision of the 1979 federal decree on domestic sugar economy.
As of September 1984, the levy on imported sugar is set to be increased from 1.70–17 Swiss francs to 3.30–33 Swiss francs per 100 kg as part of the proposed revision of the federal decree on domestic sugar economy.
If the proposed increase in the levy on imported sugar, as proposed in September 1984, is implemented, consumers will be additionally burdened by at most 15 centimes per kilo of sugar in normal cases, corresponding to additional annual expenses of about 6 Swiss francs per person (based on average consumption of 40 kg per person per year).
If the proposed increase in the levy on imported sugar, as proposed in September 1984, is implemented, in exceptional cases the levy may be increased by up to 50%, leading to additional annual expenses of about 9 Swiss francs per person (based on average consumption of 40 kg per person per year).
In the medium term (from 1984), significantly higher world sugar prices cannot be expected.
As of September 1984, the expansion of the existing Aarberg and Frauenfeld sugar factories will suffice to process the additional domestic sugar beet production, so no third sugar factory is needed.
As of September 1984, the total processing capacity of the Aarberg and Frauenfeld sugar factories can be expanded to about 16,000 tonnes of sugar beets per day within a useful time frame, at an additional investment of about 35 million Swiss francs.
As of September 1984, a third sugar factory would require an investment of about 185 million Swiss francs (excluding land purchase) for a daily capacity of about 2,000 tonnes.
As of September 1984, the quantity of contract sugar beets is to be limited to at most 1 million tonnes.
As of September 1984, additional yield-related sugar beets up to 10% of the contract quantity will be accepted at a price of two-thirds of the full price.
As of September 1984, any further sugar beets beyond that will be accepted at only 30% of the full price.
As of September 1984, the infrastructure of both the Aarberg and Frauenfeld sugar factories is designed such that a further expansion to about 8,000 tonnes per day per factory could be carried out in a relatively short time if necessary.
If domestic sugar beet production in Switzerland is expanded to 1 million tonnes under the new sugar regime to be prepared by 1985, about 90 million Swiss francs per year will need to be raised to cover negative differences in the Swiss sugar regime, compared with about 75 million francs at 850,000 tonnes.
Under the new Swiss sugar regime to be prepared by 1985, a ceiling of about 90 million francs will normally be sufficient to cover negative differences in the domestic sugar economy.
Under the proposed new Swiss sugar regime, the Swiss Confederation will be relieved of at most 20 million Swiss francs per year in normal circumstances compared with the 1979 sugar decree.
Under the proposed new Swiss sugar regime, the Swiss Confederation will be relieved of at most 25 million Swiss francs per year in exceptional circumstances compared with the 1979 sugar decree.
Expanding domestic sugar beet production by 150,000 tonnes under the new sugar regime to be prepared by 1985 will cause annual Swiss customs revenue losses of 4.5 to 5 million francs.
Under the proposed new Swiss sugar regime to be prepared by 1985, the levy on imported sugar and the additional proceeds from domestic sugar will be raised from 17 to 33 Swiss francs per 100 kg of sugar.
If the coupled federal and producer payments under the new Swiss sugar regime to be prepared by 1985 are raised by the maximum 50%, an additional 45 million francs can be raised, bringing the total to about 135 million francs.
Under the proposed new Swiss sugar regime, consumers will pay at most an additional 15 centimes per kilogram of sugar compared with the current 1979 sugar decree, corresponding to additional expenses of at most 6 Swiss francs per person per year at average consumption of about 40 kg.
Exceptionally, under the new Swiss sugar regime to be prepared by 1985, the additional consumer cost may be increased by 50%, i.e. by 3 Swiss francs per person per year.
Under the new Swiss sugar regime to be prepared by 1985, the Confederation will cover only about 5.5% of the costs in normal circumstances, compared with about one third previously.
As of September 1984, substitution of crystal sugar by glucose-derived syrups in Switzerland will become possible at the earliest once the price of crystal sugar exceeds about 150 Swiss francs and is about 50% higher than the price of isomerose or isoglucose.
As of September 1984, if plants for the enzymatic conversion of glucose into fructose-containing syrups are ever built in Switzerland, they will certainly be built only in a limited number.
Under the new Swiss sugar regime to be prepared by 1985, future allocation will be based solely on the quantity contractually agreed with each producer, and there will be no offsetting against shortfalls.
The Swiss federal decree amending the domestic sugar economy will enter into force at the beginning of a sugar year, on 1 October 1984.
From 1 October 1984, under the amended Swiss federal decree on the domestic sugar economy, the total annual quantity of contract sugar beets for which Swiss sugar factories may conclude growing contracts will not exceed 1 million tonnes.
From 1 October 1984, the amended Swiss sugar decree will stop limiting domestic sugar production by area and will rely on the quantity of contract beets instead.
From 1 October 1984, the maximum annual Swiss federal contribution to the sugar equalization fund will be reduced from 25 million CHF to 5 million CHF.
From 1 October 1984, the additional Swiss federal contribution to the sugar equalization fund will be reduced from a range of 1.5–15 million CHF to a range of 0.5–5 million CHF.
From 1 October 1984, the levy on imported sugar under the Swiss domestic sugar economy decree will be increased to 3.30–33 CHF per 100 kg.
From 1 October 1984, sugar beet growers in Switzerland will pay a cost contribution of 6–60 centimes per 100 kg of contract and additional beets under the amended decree.
The Swiss federal advance contribution to the sugar equalization fund of 10 million CHF (7.5 million CHF in 1981–1985) will be abolished.
As of September 1984, the larger domestic sugar production will cause Swiss customs revenue losses of 4.5–5 million CHF.
From 1 October 1984, the Swiss cantons and communes will not be financially affected by the amendment to the domestic sugar economy decree.
From 1 October 1984, the proposed amendment to the Swiss domestic sugar economy decree will have no effect on the staffing levels of the federal government, cantons or communes.
From 1 October 1984, under the amended Swiss sugar decree, Swiss sugar factories will be allowed to accept additional yield-related beets beyond the quantities specified in their growing contracts.
From 1 October 1984, under the amended Swiss sugar decree, sugar factories will be able to accept up to 10% of additional beets at a price equal to two thirds of the Federal Council's price for contract beets.
From 1 October 1984, under the amended Swiss sugar decree, additional beets beyond the first 10% will be purchased at 30% of the contract beet price.
The amended Swiss federal decree on the domestic sugar economy will expire when the 1979 sugar decree expires.

Switzerland's annual contribution to the Universal Postal Union will not exceed 35,000 Swiss francs under the new expenditure ceiling after the convention enters into force in 1953.
Under the new Universal Postal Convention, letters, business papers, printed matter, relief prints for the blind, merchandise samples and small packets will have to measure at least 10 cm by 7 cm, and postal administrations will have until 1 July 1955 (two years after the convention enters into force on 1 July 1953) to apply these minimum dimensions.
As of November 1952, the Universal Postal Union publication L'Union postale will henceforth be published in German in addition to French, English, Spanish, Russian, Arabic and Chinese.
The Universal Postal Convention signed in Brussels on 11 July 1952 will enter into force on 1 July 1953 and will remain in force indefinitely.
The Arrangement concerning letters and boxes with declared value, signed at Brussels on 11 July 1952, will enter into force on 1 July 1953.
The Arrangement concerning postal parcels signed at Brussels on 11 July 1952 will be put into effect on 1 July 1953.
As of November 1952, the road between Colón and Panama will be put into service.
The Arrangement concerning postal money orders and travel postal coupons, signed in Brussels on 11 July 1952, will enter into force on 1 July 1953 and remain in force for an indefinite period.
The Arrangement concerning postal transfers (virements postaux), concluded in Brussels on 11 July 1952, will enter into force on 1 July 1953.
Subject to ratification, the arrangement concerning cash on delivery (envois contre remboursement) concluded at Brussels on 11 July 1952 will come into effect on 1 July 1953.
Subject to ratification, the arrangement concerning collections (recouvrements) concluded at Brussels on 11 July 1952 will come into effect on 1 July 1953.
The Universal Postal Union arrangement on subscriptions to newspapers and periodicals, signed in Brussels on 11 July 1952, will be put into effect on 1 July 1953 and will remain in force for an indefinite period.
As of July 1975, the 200 francs placed in a savings account by Jean-Joseph Berset-Muller will, through compound interest, grow to a total of 24 million Swiss francs in about 300 years, around 2175.

The 1969 assembly of the Swiss First League (Première Ligue) will be held in Zug.
As of October 1968, the Swiss First League will propose Karl Buse of Old Boys Basel to replace Albert Stadelmann as central treasurer of the Swiss Football Association (ASF).

In the 2004-05 Swiss unihockey season, La Chaux-de-Fonds will enter two women's teams in the second league.
The Swiss Confederation will continue to provide financial support for the creation of daytime childcare facilities after 31 January 2019.
The Swiss Army reform 'Armee 95' will be implemented in Switzerland on 1 January 1995.
The legal bases necessary for the 'Armee 95' reform in the areas of military service obligation, training services and army structure will enter into force in Switzerland on 1 January 1995, if necessary through federal decrees C and D.
If the Swiss Military Law cannot enter into force on 1 January 1995, Federal Decrees C and D will be brought into force, and they will be repealed once the Military Law takes effect.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, military service obligation for captains, subaltern officers, non-commissioned officers, corporals and soldiers will be lowered to age 42.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, military service obligation for the remaining officers will be lowered to age 52.
The new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995 will fix the service obligation in law while granting the Federal Council some flexibility.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, refresher courses will generally take place every two years.
The new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995 will include not only the duties but also the rights of members of the army.
A single military ombudsperson will be appointed by the Swiss Federal Council under the new Military Law planned for 1 January 1995.
The military ombudsperson's accountability report will be published under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995.
The new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995 will introduce an assistance service under which the army can provide all forms of help to civil authorities in exceptional situations when their own means are exhausted.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, the Federal Assembly will approve assistance-service deployments involving more than 2,000 members of the army or lasting longer than three weeks.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, active service will be limited to national defence service and order service.
The new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995 will limit the number of military training sites maintained by the Confederation and the cantons to 40.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, buildings and installations serving national defence may be built, modified or converted to another military purpose only with a federal permit.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, cantons, communes, directly affected private persons and nationwide environmental organisations will have a right to be heard and to appeal in the federal permit procedure for military buildings and installations.
Under the new Swiss Military Law planned for 1 January 1995, the Federal Assembly will set the army's basic structure and organisational principles, while the Federal Council will regulate the structure of large formations and the number of troop bodies and formations.
The new Swiss Army Organisation Decree planned for 1 January 1995 will reduce the army's target strength to 400,000 members.
As of November 1993, it will take many years before a European collective security system changes decisively.
As of November 1993, protection and security within Switzerland will remain a federal responsibility in the future.
With the introduction of the Swiss Army 95 reform in 1995, the Swiss Military Women's Service and the Red Cross Service will cease to be separate elements of the Swiss Army, and female army members will be fully integrated into the troop branches or service branches in which they serve.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform in 1995, female members of the Swiss Army will continue not to receive combat assignments and will not be obligated for weapons use.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform in 1995, where training permits, women in the Swiss Army will perform shorter military services than men.
As of November 1993, under the planned Swiss military law revision, Swiss citizens who have lived or stayed abroad continuously for six years will as a rule no longer be assigned to the Swiss Army if they return to Switzerland and will instead be available for civil defense.
In the years after November 1993, Switzerland will likely have to conclude increasing numbers of bilateral agreements on the recognition of military service performed by dual citizens.
As of November 1993, under the planned Swiss military law revision, Switzerland will recognize, as fulfillment of military duties abroad, not only military service but also substitute payments in money or civilian substitute service.
As of November 1993, the maximum age for Swiss military recruitment (Aushebung) will be lowered from 28 to 25.
As of November 1993, the planned Swiss military law revision will in future make electronic data capture possible for the Swiss military service booklet.
As of November 1993, the Swiss military service booklet will in future also be usable for civil defense, civilian service, and service in civil leadership organs of overall defense.
As of November 1993, under the revision, a person who does not follow an invitation to receive information about Swiss military recruitment will no longer be punished.
As of November 1993, the end of military service liability for Swiss NCOs, privates, soldiers, captains, subaltern officers, and specialist officers in the corresponding function will be set at age 42.
As of November 1993, captains and specialist officers in the corresponding function will remain liable for Swiss military service until age 52 if needed.
As of November 1993, higher staff officers, staff officers, and specialist officers in the corresponding function will remain liable for Swiss military service until age 52.
As of November 1993, if needed, the Swiss military service liability of higher staff officers will last until age 62.
As of November 1993, for special needs of the Swiss Army and other parts of overall defense, military service liability will be extended to age 52.
As of November 1993, persons already released from Swiss military service because they reached the age limit will not be reassigned even if an increase in the age limit would bring them back into a liable cohort.
As of November 1993, a Swiss Army member may be used beyond the Article 13 age limits only with his consent, with an upper age limit of 65.
As of November 1993, in the list of persons exempted from Swiss military service for essential activities, Vice-Chancellors and full-time members of rescue services and military services (fire, oil, and chemical brigades) will be newly included.
As of November 1993, a person will be exempted from Swiss military service if he exercises the activity leading to exemption for an average of at least 35 hours per week.
As of November 1993, completion of recruit school will be required for all persons liable for Swiss military service except federal magistrates and clergy.
As of November 1993, under the revision, there will be a general waiver of reassignment to the Swiss Army after six years of service exemption.
As of November 1993, the current Article 16 of the Swiss Military Organization, providing exclusion from military service for unworthiness, will be repealed.
As of November 1993, a foreign criminal court judgment may be used as a ground for examining exclusion from Swiss military service.
As of November 1993, the revision will create a legal basis for obtaining police conduct reports in Swiss military service matters.
As of November 1993, the revision will newly establish the obligation of Swiss military service members to ensure they can be reached while off duty.
As of November 1993, in future, the possibility of police bringing in persons for violations of Swiss military reporting duties will be waived.
As of November 1993, a revision of the Swiss federal law on income compensation for persons serving in the army and civil defense will improve income replacement and mitigate social hardship in training for cadre functions in the army.
As of November 1993, the revision will enshrine in law the principle that Swiss Army members retain constitutional and statutory rights during military service.
As of November 1993, the revised law will newly mention psychological and social counseling and support for Swiss Army members.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, the maximum service obligation for Gefreite and soldiers will be set at 330 days.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, recruit school will generally last 103 days.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, the maximum age for completing recruit school will be lowered by three years.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, most of the army will hold 19-day repetition courses every two years.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, certain formations, including air force and anti-aircraft troops, mobilization organs, and staffs and parts of staff battalions of large formations, will hold annual 12-day repetition courses.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, a five-day Tactical-Technical Course (TTK) for all officers, including junior officers, and staff adjutants will be held in years without repetition courses and every second year for formations with annual repetition courses.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, non-commissioned officer school will generally last 40 days.
Under the Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, officer school will generally last 117 days.
Under the planned Swiss Army 95 reform, to be introduced in 1995, cadre preliminary courses lasting at most five days will be held Monday to Friday.
As of November 1993, a single Military Ombudsperson for the Swiss Army will be appointed by the Federal Council.
As of November 1993, under the revised Swiss Military Organization Act, general mandatory AIDS tests will not be ordered on the basis of this provision.
In the Army 95 reform in 1995, the function of Generaladjutant will no longer be provided for in the Swiss Army.
As of November 1993, in the planned revision of the Swiss Military Act, the term 'Heer' will no longer be used and will be replaced by 'Armee'.
As of November 1993, the current army staff units responsible for Federal Council tasks, such as the Press and Radio Section (APF) and the National Alarm Centre (NAZ), will become staffs of the Federal Council.
As of November 1993, a legal basis at federal law level will be created for the Fortress Guard Corps and the Surveillance Squadron.
As of November 1993, members of the Military Women's Service (MFD) and the Red Cross Service (RKD) will no longer be double-registered administratively.
As of November 1993, within the Training Group, an office specifically for the concerns of female members of the Army will remain in place.
As of November 1993, the Corps of General Staff Officers will be anchored in law.
As of November 1993, the planned target strength of the Swiss Army will be reduced to at most 400,000 military service personnel in formations.
Over the next 10 years from 1993, the effective strength of the Army 95 will be rapidly reduced, leading to a reduction of overstrengths and an ever smaller manoeuvre mass.
In the Army 95 reform in 1995, the alarm formations Infantry Regiment Geneva, Infantry Regiment Bern, and the Airport Regiment will be composed of members of combat troops from various branches.
In the Army 95 reform in 1995, each of the alarm formations Infantry Regiment Geneva, Infantry Regiment Bern, and the Airport Regiment will be assigned a command staff rather than a troop unit staff.
As of November 1993, a separation will be created between the military security service and the military intelligence service, and between the military services and the civilian defense and intelligence service, with clear regulation of information exchange.
As of November 1993, the exceptions provided in Article 24 of the Data Protection Act will become obsolete with the enactment of the new Military Act provisions, and the necessary adjustments will be made.
As of November 1993, person security checks will find their legal basis in the future Federal Act on the Preservation of Internal Security.
In the Army 95 in 1995, the rank of Stabsadjutant will be newly introduced as a non-commissioned officer rank.
In the Army 95 in 1995, the Stabsadjutant in the troops will be assigned exclusively in the staffs of battalions and detachments.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that grade years as a condition for military promotion are dropped, as stipulated by the Federal Council in the promotion conditions.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that military promotion will in future be carried out when the officer has passed the prescribed services (promotion services, functional or command years) and takes over the new command or function.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that appointment as a specialist officer will no longer be necessarily revoked when the corresponding officer function is no longer exercised.
As of November 1993, the Swiss Federal Council will have to specify the military buildings and installations in question in its implementing ordinance to the revised Military Organization Act.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will explicitly mention community or regional shooting facilities, which have not previously appeared in the law.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will introduce a public and formal federal procedure for the construction of military buildings and installations, aligned with the procedure applicable to civil buildings.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will introduce participation and appeal rights for affected third parties in the procedure for military construction.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will give the Swiss Federal Council the competence to exempt installations under the Federal Act on the Protection of Military Installations from the permit requirement.
As of November 1993, the Swiss Federal Council will shape the hearing right and may regulate it differently for buildings and installations not subject to the permit requirement (Article 131(4)) in the interest of secrecy.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that formally only a single permit is issued for military construction, subject to the consent of the federal authorities competent under other federal legislation.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will ensure that the requirements of substantive environmental law are fully taken into account within the military construction permit procedure.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will require that approval from the Swiss Federal Office for the Environment, Forests and Landscape (BUWAL) be obtained for clearings of up to 5000 m2 of forest, which under the Forest Act are generally subject to cantonal approval.
As of November 1993, Article 22(3) of the Swiss Nature and Heritage Protection Act will be formulated more comprehensively to also include military projects in the permit requirement for the removal of riparian vegetation.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will ensure that the cantons can safeguard their interests, for example in the area of forest law: they will be heard before the decision is issued and will receive the right of appeal.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will give the cantons and communes a formal right of appeal against the permit decision, in addition to the right to be heard.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will make it possible to combine the military permit procedure with an expropriation procedure.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will reduce the current provision on accommodation for troops to the current needs of the troops.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will newly mention parking spaces in Article 134, as in practice no compensation is generally paid for them.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will oblige municipalities to ensure that suitable shooting ranges are available free of charge for their shooting-duty personnel, either by building such facilities themselves or by enabling access to a foreign shooting range; if this cannot be realised, the cantonal military authority may assign a foreign municipal shooting range, order the merger of several municipalities into a special-purpose association for the construction of a joint shooting range, or order the construction of a municipal shooting range on the territory of another municipality.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that for strict liability it suffices if a single member of the armed forces causes the damage (e.g., by firing a shot).
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will also cover pure economic loss under the liability provisions.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will expressly declare paragraphs 2-4 of Article 137 applicable to both liability provisions (Articles 22(1) and 23 MO combined).
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that the federal government's liability for damages from off-duty activities of military associations is subsidiary.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will insert Articles 49, 51, 52, and 53 of the Swiss Code of Obligations into Article 27(1) on liability principles.
As of November 1993, the revised Swiss Military Organization Act will provide that the statute of limitations for the federal government's recourse or damage participation claim begins with knowledge of the damage and of the liable party.
As of November 1993, the Swiss Army's Redoubt and Border Brigades will be dissolved as part of the planned revision of the Military Act.
As of November 1993, five tank brigades will be formed from the existing three mechanized divisions of the Swiss Army.
