As of November 1993, a disaster relief regiment with four decentralised battalions will be formed in the Swiss Army.
As of November 1993, the Swiss Army's territorial zones will be reorganised into four territorial divisions and two territorial brigades, and territorial districts will be renamed territorial regiments.
As of November 1993, the basic structure of the six field divisions of the Swiss Army will be retained.
As of November 1993, each Swiss Army field division will consist of three infantry regiments, one artillery regiment, two tank battalions and several independent battalions or detachments.
As of November 1993, the basic structure of the three Swiss Army mountain divisions will be retained, but each will have only two mountain infantry regiments instead of the previous number.
As of November 1993, each Swiss Army corps will be assigned one territorial division, and the Mountain Army Corps will additionally be assigned two territorial brigades.
As of November 1993, military leave travel for Swiss Army members will be free of charge (no ticket required) under the revised Military Act.
As of November 1993, exemptions from assistant and active service in the Swiss Army will generally be granted only from the age of 30.

As of November 1993, under the Swiss Army 95 reform, the artillery forts remaining after liquidation will be successively replaced by a modern artillery system.

As of November 1993, a new field telegraph/field telephone brigade will be created to place PTT telecommunications facilities and parts of FM radio supply at the service of total defence and to maintain civilian telecommunications and other radio supply as long as possible.

As of November 1993, under the planned Swiss Military Law, at most 40% of conscripts selected for service will be allowed to be assigned to cantonal formations.
As of November 1993, with the planned authorized strength, each Swiss canton will generally be able to keep at least one of its own fusilier or rifle battalions.

Customs duties on industrial products traded between Switzerland and Spain, and between Switzerland and Portugal, will be gradually eliminated on a reciprocal basis over the seven-year transitional period starting from 1 March 1986, with full elimination by 1 January 1993.
Free trade between Switzerland and Spain will be fully realized from 1 January 1993.
The dismantling of customs duties on industrial and processed agricultural products imported into Switzerland from Spain will result, at the end of the seven-year transition period (by 1 January 1993), in a decrease in Swiss customs revenue of about 4.3 million Swiss francs per year.
The reintroduction or increase of customs duties on certain agricultural products will result in an increase in Swiss customs revenue of about 0.6 million Swiss francs per year from the entry into force of the agreement on 1 March 1986.
As of September 1986, for Portugal, the reintroduction of customs duties on certain industrial products and processed agricultural products and the elimination of tariff preferences previously granted by Switzerland on certain agricultural products will result in an increase in Swiss customs revenue of about 0.8 million Swiss francs per year.
As of September 1986, the results of the negotiation will have no effect on the staffing levels of the Swiss federal administration.
From 1986 onward, export conditions for Swiss industrial products and processed agricultural products to Spain will become more favorable, while those to Portugal will remain practically unchanged.
As of September 1986, the net loss of Swiss customs revenue resulting from the application of the free trade agreements to Spain and Portugal will be about 3 million Swiss francs per year, which is minimal.
Spain will maintain quantitative import quotas for fourteen product groups with respect to EFTA countries (including Switzerland) for a transitional period of three or four years, starting from 1 March 1986.
The global quotas fixed for imports from EFTA countries will safeguard Swiss export flows to Spain in 1986 and subsequent years.
As of September 1986, the annual rate of increase of these quotas will subsequently be the same as that applicable to products from the ten EC member states.
As of September 1986, the passive processing regime with Portugal for Swiss fabrics will be subject to the same rules as those governing Switzerland's relations in this area with the other EC member states.
As of September 1986, the provisions of the Acts of Accession that limit textile imports into Spain will not apply to Switzerland.

As of September 1986, a duty-free quota of 1000 tonnes of table cherries (excluding sour cherries) will be granted by the European Community to Switzerland.
As of September 1986, Spain's adoption of the Community regime will improve Switzerland's position on the Spanish market.
As of September 1986, the arrangement concluded for Portugal will safeguard an acceptable competitive position for Switzerland relative to the EC member states.

The ordinance on the Swiss Federal Insurance Fund (Eidgenössische Versicherungskasse, EVK) and the statutes of the Pension and Assistance Fund of the Swiss Federal Railways (Pensions- und Hilfskasse der SBB, PHK) will enter into force on 1 January 1988, subject to approval by the Federal Chambers.
As of May 1987, the reorganization of the EVK and PHK will not cause additional costs for employers in the medium term.
As of May 1987, the increase in recurring contributions to the EVK and PHK by 1.5 percentage points of insured salary will be offset in the medium term by the elimination of employer participation in buy-ins, by members financing buy-ins, and by higher interest on account balances.
Employee contributions to the EVK and PHK will increase by one quarter, from 6% to 7.5% of insured salary, when the new statutes enter into force on 1 January 1988.
Under the new EVK and PHK statutes planned to enter into force in 1988, about 35% of insured persons, spread across five age cohorts, will make use of early retirement in addition to invalidations.
As of May 1987, the number of part-time jobs in Switzerland will increase for various reasons.
As of May 1987, under the new EVK statutes, the Swiss Confederation will guarantee the Eidgenössische Versicherungskasse (EVK) an interest yield equal to the average yield on federal bonds, but at least 4% per year.
As of May 1987, the transfer of the Pensions- und Hilfskasse der Schweizerischen Bundesbahnen (PHK) into the Eidgenössische Versicherungskasse (EVK) will be postponed to a later date.
As of May 1987, a pension scheme will be created for members of the Swiss federal parliament, allowing an appropriate lump-sum payment when they leave parliamentary service.
As of May 1987, the Federal Chancellor of Switzerland will no longer be insured with the EVK, and the Federal Act on the salary of the Federal Chancellor will be supplemented with a provision on retirement pay.
As of May 1987, a new pension scheme guaranteeing benefits under the BVG will be created for military tailor home workers in Switzerland.
As of May 1987, the medical service's decision on whether new entrants to the EVK are admitted with or without a reservation will be abolished, because no reservation will be made for insurance.
Over the 14 years from 1987 to 2001, the financial burden on the Swiss Confederation and its enterprises (PTT and SBB) for the EVK and PHK pension funds will not increase proportionally; it will decline somewhat.
As of May 1987, the recurring contributions to the Swiss Federal Pension Fund (EVK) will be increased from 12% to 15%, with the increase split equally between employees and employer.
As of May 1987, the special provision of the Federal Employees Act allowing claims arising from the insurance relationship to be brought directly before the Federal Court will be repealed as part of the revision of the Federal Law on the Organization of Federal Justice (OG).
In 1987, all registered pension institutions in Switzerland will pay a contribution of 0.2% of the sum of coordinated salaries under the BVG (compulsory occupational pension law) to the Sicherheitsfonds.
In 1987, the Eidgenössische Versicherungskasse (EVK), the Swiss federal pension fund, will pay approximately 6 million Swiss francs to the Sicherheitsfonds.
As of May 1987, the existing statutes of the Eidgenössische Versicherungskasse (EVK) will be completely repealed when the revised statutes enter into force.
As of May 1987, under the revised EVK statutes, the disability pension graduated by insurance years will be abolished, and the EVK disability pension will generally be 60% of the insured salary at the time of invalidation.
As of May 1987, the Swiss Confederation will pay interest above 4% on EVK funds it manages if the average yield on federal bonds exceeds 4%.
In 1995, the coverage ratio of the Swiss Federal Insurance Fund (EVK) will be only insignificantly lower than in 1984.
In 1995, the coverage ratio of the Pension and Provident Fund of the Swiss Federal Railways (PHK) will be about 1.5 percent lower than in 1984.
Employer contributions to the EVK and PHK will increase by about one quarter by 1995.
As of May 1987, under the new pension-fund model, employees will pay 2.6 to 2.8 percent of insured salary more than before, including earnings-increase contributions for inflation allowances.
When the 1985 model is introduced, the employer burden at the EVK will remain roughly the same as under the previous system.
Under the new model, the EVK's coverage ratio will increase by about one fifth by 1999.
As of May 1987, under the Federal Council's model, the cost burden on employees at the EVK will rise by about 2.5 percent.
As of May 1987, under the Federal Council's model, the cost burden on employees at the PHK will rise by about 2 percent.
As of May 1987, if the inflation rate changes by 2 percentage points, employer contributions will vary by about 5 percent.
If the fund's assets earn 5 percent instead of 4 percent, employer contributions will be about 2.5 percent of insured salary lower by 1999 under the proposed financing model.
As of May 1987, an increase in employee contributions totaling 2.5 percent of insured salary will finance flexible retirement and various other benefit improvements while the employer contribution rate remains roughly constant.
By 1999, the cost split between employees and employers in the EVK and PHK will shift from about 30:70 to 34:66.
Federal contributions to the EVK and PHK will decrease slightly by 1999, by about CHF 20 million.
As of May 1987, EVK-affiliated enterprises with their own accounts (PTT, armament enterprises and the Alcohol Administration) and affiliated organisations will be relieved by about CHF 28 million.
As of May 1987, the additional burden on the Swiss Federal Railways (SBB) from the new PHK statutes will be only short-term, and the planned granting of mortgage loans will not restrict the financing of SBB investments.
Over the five years following May 1987, about 3,700 of the roughly 10,400 federal employees aged 60 to 64 will claim early retirement, creating an additional recruitment need of about 750 people per year.
As of May 1987, introducing the new statutes will temporarily increase the staff of the EVK and PHK by two persons each.
Article 20 of the statutes of the Swiss Federal Insurance Fund (EVK) will be phased in for members born in 1923-1932 between 1988 and 1992.
The statutes of the Pensions and Relief Fund of the Swiss Federal Railways will enter into force on 1 January 1988.
Article 20 of the Pensions and Relief Fund of the Swiss Federal Railways statutes will be brought into force in stages for members born in 1923-1932 between 1988 and 1992.

The 1959 International Wheat Agreement, concluded in Geneva on 10 March 1959, will enter into force on 1 August 1959, provided that it is signed by at least two thirds of the countries that participated in the Geneva negotiations.

The new French end-of-life law to be voted in 2015 will include three axes: access to palliative care, taking into account the patient’s wishes, and the possibility of “terminal sedation” administered to terminally ill people with unrelievable pain.
The right to sedation will be strictly regulated in the text of the French end-of-life law submitted to a vote in 2015.

In German-speaking Switzerland on the morning of 3 March 1977, there will be fog banks in the plains, followed by fairly sunny weather.
South of the Alps and in Engadine on 3 March 1977, the weather will be generally fine, with afternoon temperatures around 15°C.

In southern Switzerland on Friday 4 March 1977, the weather will be sometimes cloudy; on Saturday 5 March 1977 it will be sunny, and the weather will be very mild throughout Switzerland.

Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the existing recourse commission for water management (active since 1 January 1994) and the recourse commissions for postal and telecommunications (established on 1 January 1998) will be integrated into the new UVEK recourse commission.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the new UVEK recourse commission will have a minimum of seven judges.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the new UVEK recourse commission will have an annual caseload of approximately 150 to 180 appeals.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), an additional salary cost of approximately 0.7 to 0.9 million Swiss francs will arise.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the time required to resolve an individual dispute before the new UVEK recourse commission will decrease compared to the current system.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the creation of the new UVEK recourse commission will relieve the Swiss Federal Court.
The Swiss federal decree on the plan approval procedure for major railway projects (BB EGP) will remain unchanged until its expiry on 31 December 2000.
For plan approval procedures under the BB EGP that are not completed by 31 December 2000, a special regulation in the Swiss Railway Act (EBG) will maintain the essential procedural advantages: the UVEK will remain the first-instance plan approval authority, and the appeal route to the Federal Court will be maintained.
The special regulation for plan approval procedures under the BB EGP that are not completed by 31 December 2000 will enter into force on 31 December 2000, simultaneously with the expiry of the BB EGP.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), a subsidiary federal competence will be created to approve temporary interim storage for excavated material when no legally binding cantonal landfill permit exists at the time of plan approval.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the delegation of assessment competence from the Federal Commission for Nature and Heritage Protection (ENHK) to specialist federal agencies will be expanded.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the Federal Commission for Nature and Heritage Protection (ENHK) will only assess projects that raise concerns of a significant intervention in an inventory object or that raise fundamental questions of nature and landscape protection.
As of May 1998, the amendments to the various Swiss federal laws concerning plan approval procedures will be summarized in a single omnibus act and subjected as a whole to a referendum.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), a legal regulation in the Expropriation Act will ensure that the expropriation title is available beyond the actual route also for land needed for environmental compensation measures.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), competence delegations that can be integrated into the legal changes of VKB project No. 2 will also be included in the omnibus application.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the new UVEK recourse commission will be used as appeal instance in some decrees not only in the area of concessions or plan approvals, but in all areas.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), the new UVEK recourse commission will not decide as last instance for plan approval decisions and other decisions of special weight.
Under the proposed Swiss federal law on coordination and simplification of plan approval procedures (May 1998), an adaptation of Articles 99 and 100 of the Swiss Federal Judiciary Act (OG) will guarantee the appeal route to the Federal Court for plan approval decisions and other decisions of special weight.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Trolleybus Act will refer to the plan approval procedure of the Railway Act (EBG) and will not contain its own procedure.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Pipeline Act will require only a plan approval from the supervisory authority for the construction and modification of pipeline facilities, and will abolish the concession requirement.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Pipeline Act will remove the restriction that only Swiss citizens or Swiss-controlled legal entities may obtain a concession for cross-border pipelines.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Aviation Act will introduce the hearing model (Anhörungsmodell) and combine project approval and building concession procedures.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Aviation Act will concentrate decision-making authority in a single authority.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Aviation Act will introduce an appeal commission (Rekurskommission) for aviation matters.
As of May 1998, the revised Swiss Aviation Act will abolish the building concession for airports and integrate all aspects previously examined in the building concession procedure into the plan approval procedure; only an operating concession will be required for airports.
As of May 1998, a sectoral plan 'Infrastructure of Aviation' (Sachplan Infrastruktur der Luftfahrt) will be prepared for buildings and facilities in the aviation sector.
As of May 1998, under the revised Swiss Aviation Act, the hearing of municipalities and other affected parties will be conducted directly by the Federal Office (Bundesamt) instead of by the cantons.
As of May 1998, the concentration of procedures at the federal level will be retained in the other environmental laws beyond the special areas covered by VKB project No. 2.
As of May 1998, the Federal Council's competence to issue special provisions for areas where the envisaged enforcement organisation is unsuitable will be retained in the Environmental Protection Act (USG) and will also be included in the Water Protection Act (GSchG) and the Biotechnology Act (BGF).
As of May 1998, the Federal Office for the Environment (BUWAL) will be granted a right of appeal for the cantonal implementation of the Environmental Protection Act (USG), the Water Protection Act (GSchG) and the Biotechnology Act (BGF).

If the proposed 1998 Swiss legislation on the coordination and simplification of planning approval procedures is enacted, the elimination of concession procedures for pipeline installations will cause an annual loss of fee revenue of up to 100,000 Swiss francs, only partly offset by additional revenue from planning approval procedures.

Switzerland will be admitted to the Inter-American Development Bank (BID) alongside eleven other non-regional countries by 1976.
Switzerland will subscribe 13.752 million dollars (current value) to the Inter-American Development Bank's (BID) interregional capital, with one sixth of this amount payable in three installments between 1976 and 1978.
Switzerland will contribute 13.752 million dollars to the Inter-American Development Bank's (BID) Fund for Special Operations (FOS), payable in three equal installments in 1976, 1977, and 1978.
The Inter-American Development Bank (BID) will increase its ordinary capital and reconstitute its Fund for Special Operations (FOS) starting in 1976.
As of August 1975, Switzerland will grant Cameroon a loan of 6 million Swiss francs to finance the construction of a bridge over the Sanaga River near Koro.
As of August 1975, Cameroon will build a bridge over the Sanaga River near Koro with the aim of stimulating development in the surrounding regions.
As of August 1975, Cameroon will provide an essential contribution to the bridge project over the Sanaga River near Koro, including building access roads on both sides of the river to connect the new bridge to the existing road network.

As of August 1975, if the extra-regional countries subscribe 311 million dollars to the BID's interregional capital, they will hold about 5.5% of the bank's total capital and 5% of voting rights.
As of August 1975, under the BID's new statutes, Latin American member countries will continue to hold a permanent majority of votes.
As of August 1975, Venezuela will provide 500 million dollars to a special fund managed by the Inter-American Development Bank (BID).
As of August 1975, the Inter-American Development Bank's ordinary capital will be increased by raising the callable subscription of its 22 Latin American member countries by 439.99 million current US dollars, bringing the bank's total capital to about 6.76 billion current US dollars.
When extra-regional countries join the Inter-American Development Bank by 31 December 1976, they will be collectively represented by two executive directors and their alternates on the bank's Board of Directors, whose membership will increase from nine to eleven.
The approval procedure by the regional member countries' governments or parliaments for the three Inter-American Development Bank resolutions on membership revision, admission norms and capital increase will be completed before the end of March 1976.
As of August 1975, the competent national authorities of the regional Inter-American Development Bank member countries will approve the amendments to Article III that would allow the Caribbean Development Bank to be a beneficiary of Inter-American Development Bank loans.
As of August 1975, suppliers from extra-regional Inter-American Development Bank member countries will be allowed to participate in tenders for Inter-American Development Bank Special Operations Fund projects to the extent that those projects are financed by contributions from industrialized donor countries.
If Switzerland joins the Inter-American Development Bank, its subscription of 11.484 million 1959 US dollars will give it about 3.7% of the initial interregional capital and about 0.2% of the bank's new total capital.
If Switzerland joins the Inter-American Development Bank, 48 million Swiss francs will suffice by 1978 to cover its payments to the bank's capital and Special Operations Fund.
If Switzerland joins the Inter-American Development Bank, the remaining 18 million Swiss francs charged to the financial aid programme will be paid in three instalments of 6 million Swiss francs each between 1976 and 1978.
After August 1975, Latin America will probably become more important, both as a market for Swiss exports and as a production zone for agricultural goods and raw materials imported by Switzerland.

If the fall in prices and incomes from Cameroon's main export products continues in the years after 1975, exports will not resume their role as the main engine of Cameroon's development.
The fundamental options of Cameroon's Fourth Five-Year Plan (1976/77-1980/81) will probably not be changed.
Cameroon's Fourth Five-Year Plan (1976/77-1980/81) will increase public investments in agriculture and rural development.
Cameroon's Fourth Five-Year Plan (1976/77-1980/81) will allocate an important share of public investments to the development of transport and communications infrastructure.

As of August 1975, the loan agreement between Switzerland and Cameroon for the Sanaga bridge near Koro will enter into force on the day the two governments notify each other that their constitutional formalities are completed.
As of August 1975, Cameroon will contribute at least 100 million CFA francs to the bridge project over the Sanaga River near Koro and will provide any additional funds needed to complete it.
As of August 1975, Cameroon will finance the full construction of bitumen or laterite access roads linking the Sanaga bridge near Koro to Saa in Lekié Department to the south and to Koro in Mbam Department to the north.
As of August 1975, the project will build a bridge over the Sanaga River near Koro with a 240-metre prefabricated single-lane metal deck and two-lane bridge infrastructure plus two stabilised access ramps.
As of August 1975, Cameroon will pay interest of 0.75% per year on the used amount of the 6 million Swiss franc loan.

As of August 1975, the proceeds of the Swiss loan to Cameroon for the Sanaga bridge near Koro will not be used to pay taxes, customs duties, fees or other charges imposed by Cameroon on goods or services related to the project.

As of September 1988, without other legislation and measures to reduce air pollutant loads, efforts to protect the forest under the draft Swiss Federal Law on Forest Conservation and Protection against Natural Hazards (Forest Law, WaG) will ultimately remain unsuccessful.
As of September 1988, the forced one-sided orientation of forest care toward containing damage and forced logging will lead to a substantial reduction of forest care according to silvicultural principles in Switzerland.
As of September 1988, the structures of Swiss forest stands will be disturbed in the long term by the one-sided focus on damage containment and forced logging, so that especially in the mountains the protective function of the forest will be called into question.
In the ten years following the 1983-1985 Swiss National Forest Inventory, the potential annual wood harvest in Swiss forests will be 6.5 million cubic metres.
The new Swiss Forest Act (Waldgesetz) will enter into force during the 1987-1991 legislative period.
As of September 1988, all old unlimited forest-clearing permits in Switzerland will lapse two years after the new Swiss Forest Act enters into force.
About ten federal positions will be needed to implement the new Swiss Forest Act, including the four positions requested in the 1987 federal decision on extraordinary measures for forest conservation.
Under the draft Swiss Forest Act presented in September 1988, additional federal expenditure of 125 million Swiss francs will arise in total.
As of September 1988, once the new Swiss Forest Act is in force, the cantons as a whole will need at least 100 million Swiss francs more per year for protection against natural hazards, use and maintenance contributions, forest-owner infrastructure, forest protection and training.
As of September 1988, the subsidy rates for individual measures under the new Swiss Forest Act will be graduated in an implementing ordinance.
As of September 1988, the increase in federal aid under the new Swiss Forest Act will be accompanied by co-financing from fuel customs revenues according to the polluter-pays principle.
As of September 1988, the increase in subsidies under the new Swiss Forest Act will increase the share of total expenditures financed according to the polluter-pays principle.

As of July 1998, the first stage of the new urban combat training facility (Ortskampfanlage) at the Äuli-Paschga shooting range on the Walenstadt military training area in the canton of St. Gallen, Switzerland, will be built, comprising buildings in the town centre, an infrastructure building and most of the road network.
As of July 1998, the measures announced in the environmental impact report for the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage will be implemented by the acceptance of the first stage at the latest.
As of July 1998, after the use concept for the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage is approved, a protection ordinance will be elaborated with the involved authorities as its concretisation.
As of July 1998, once the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage is in operation, special formations will carry out helicopter exercises on six consecutive days in August, with 2 to 3 daily landings in contour flight.
As of July 1998, after training operations begin at the new Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage, noise measurements will be carried out at the facility.
As of July 1998, the use concept for the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage will become a binding component of the building permit.
As of July 1998, the two nearest residential buildings to the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage will receive additional soundproof windows to protect their occupants.
As of July 1998, if the planned and proposed measures, in particular implementation of the use concept, are realised, the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage as a whole (stages 1 and 2) will not increase environmental pollution and can be classified as environmentally compatible.
As of July 1998, after the new KIUG facility at Walenstadt is in operation, it and the two nearest shooting ranges will experience a threefold cumulative use, while the other ranges will be used correspondingly less.
As of July 1998, in the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage, marker ammunition will be used predominantly because it produces considerably less noise than combat ammunition.
As of July 1998, as far as possible, the few planned flat roofs of the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage (garages, annexes, etc.) will be greened.
As of July 1998, the two flat roofs used for helicopter exercises at the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage will receive an artificial green covering to reduce their visibility from higher-lying residential areas.
As of July 1998, as far as user requirements allow, the roads and squares of the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage will be given permeable surfaces.
As of July 1998, some forecourts at the Walenstadt Ortskampfanlage that are not used for covering, positioning or turning manoeuvres, as well as some sidewalk areas, will additionally be given permeable surfaces.
As of July 1998, once the Ortskampfanlage (OKA) at the Walenstadt military training area in the canton of St. Gallen, Switzerland, is realised, helicopter flight movements there will amount to 6 to 10 days per year, with 10 arrivals and departures per day.
As of July 1998, under the binding maximum flight movements and specified flight route for the planned OKA at the Walenstadt military training area, noise immissions at the nearest reception point E1 will be 81 dB(A), below the applicable limit of 85 dB(A).
As of July 1998, during the approximately twelve-month construction phase of the OKA at the Walenstadt military training area, no noticeably perceptible noise burdens are expected at the nearest immission points, provided the UVB-proposed construction-noise mitigation measures are implemented.
As of July 1998, no impairment of groundwater in the project perimeter of the OKA at the Walenstadt military training area from transport of pollutants from the suspected heating-oil contamination site is expected.
As of July 1998, after training operations begin at the OKA Walenstadt, the permitting authority will have noise measurements carried out and will order any measures resulting from them.

As of November 1970, the other Rhine riparian states and Belgium are expected to ratify the 1966 Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels.
Switzerland will deposit its instruments of ratification for the 1965 Convention on the Registration of Inland Vessels and the 1966 Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels at the time it is established that the other Rhine riparian states and Belgium are ready to ratify them.
As of November 1970, legal uniformity on the Rhine under the 1960 Convention on the Unification of Certain Rules concerning Collisions of Inland Vessels will be ensured soon.
After parliamentary approval, Switzerland will accede to the 1960 Convention on the Unification of Certain Rules concerning Collisions of Inland Vessels.
If Switzerland ratifies Protocol No. 1 to the 1965 Convention on the Registration of Inland Vessels, Swiss inland vessels will no longer be subject to all the numerous and varied privileges under the laws of foreign states through which they pass, but only to the privileges listed in Article 11 of the protocol.
Upon ratification of Protocol No. 1 to the 1965 Convention on the Registration of Inland Vessels, there will be a significant reduction in the privileges that rank ahead of mortgages on inland vessels.
As of November 1970, if Swiss law adopts only the maritime liens provided in Protocol No. 1 and no additional privileges ranking ahead of mortgages, mortgage creditors of Swiss ships will not have to fear that additional liens will rank ahead of their mortgage claims in any other contracting state.
As of November 1970, the simplified procedure under Article 17(1)-(6) of the Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels of 15 February 1966 for amending the annex and appendices will hardly be used in practice; the administrative-agreement procedure under Article 17(7) will be preferred.
As of November 1970, the other Rhine riparian states and Belgium will approve the new Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels of 15 February 1966.
As of November 1970, if the other Rhine riparian states and Belgium approve the new Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels of 15 February 1966, Switzerland will also have to join it in order to maintain legal uniformity on the Rhine.
As of November 1970, the contracting states of the new Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels of 15 February 1966 will have to issue new measurement certificates according to the new model.
As of November 1970, upon ratification of or accession to the new Convention on the Measurement of Inland Vessels of 15 February 1966, Switzerland will notify the distinguishing letters “CH” to the UN Secretary-General in time.
As of November 1970, if a federal law on inland navigation is enacted in Switzerland sooner or later, collision liability will have to be aligned with the international conventions.
As of November 1970, if Switzerland accedes to the Convention for the Unification of Certain Rules concerning Collisions in Inland Navigation of 15 March 1960, the strict liability of licensed Swiss shipping will be replaced by fault liability for collision liability, and a uniform collision law will apply on all Swiss waters to all ships.

During the 1990s, Europe will still have large, powerful and modern armies equipped in conventional fashion and possessing nuclear military potentials.
The already decided reduction of chemical weapons stocks in signatory states will take about ten years, i.e. until about 2001.

Under the 1991 Swiss armament programme, Switzerland will acquire 500,000 ABC protective masks and 300,000 C protective suits, with 100,000 of the suits reserved for training, during the 1992-1995 implementation phase.
Compensatory orders awarded to Swiss industry under the 1991 armament programme will amount to about 339 million Swiss francs and will bring the employment rate for Swiss industry from the projects in the programme to about 88 percent.
As of June 1991, further acquisition of C protective suits will take place only after the structures of Army 95 have been determined and the need for C protection for all functions has been studied.
As of June 1991, a second series of new combat uniforms and combat packs will be requested in one of the next Swiss armament programmes.
As of June 1991, the new C protective equipment will be introduced in the repetition and instruction courses of the various branches of the Swiss army.
Delivery of the Swiss Army's ABC individual protective equipment (protective masks, C protective suits, gloves and boots) will begin in 1994 and continue until 1997.
A subsequent series of C protective suits will be proposed to equip the remaining Swiss troops during the 1992-1995 implementation phase.
Funding of about 350,000 Swiss francs for construction work at the Federal Arms Factory in Bern to produce correction lenses for the new ABC protective mask will be requested in the 1992 construction budget.
From 1993, the sleeping bag will be part of the personal equipment that Swiss soldiers take home.
Delivery of the Swiss Army's thermal imaging devices will take place from mid-1993 to mid-1996.
As of June 1991, once the final structures of the new Swiss Army 95 are known, the acquisition of a new series of thermal imaging devices will probably be proposed.
As of June 1991, additional ammunition for the Swiss Army's new anti-tank rocket launcher will be requested in a later armament procurement program.
Instructors of the Swiss infantry and mechanized and light troops will be trained in 1992.
From 1993 to 1996, Swiss infantry and mechanized/light troop formations will be retrained, and other troops will be retrained afterwards.
As of June 1991, the introduction of the new anti-tank rocket launcher will take place simultaneously in troop courses and in the recruit and cadre schools of the respective Swiss army corps.
As of June 1991, the C protective suits will be manufactured by two foreign companies.
As of June 1991, final assembly of the new ABC protective masks will largely be carried out by Biwi SA in Glovelier, canton of Jura, Switzerland.
As of June 1991, correction lenses for the new ABC protective mask will be supplied by the Federal Arms Factory in Bern.
As of June 1991, sixteen new jobs will be created for the preparation and maintenance of the ABC individual protective equipment.
As of June 1991, five additional jobs will be created at the War Material Supply Office to ensure maintenance and filling of gas bottles for the thermal imaging devices.
As of June 1991, construction work costing about 0.3 million Swiss francs will be necessary for storage, maintenance and training for the thermal imaging devices.
After June 1991, the Swiss Army will use shooting simulators similar to those successfully used for the Panzer 68 and Leopard 87 tanks and for the Dragon and TOW guided weapons for anti-tank rocket launcher training.
As of June 1991, a limited number of reserve anti-tank rocket launchers will be allocated to the troops and to repair organs of the War Material Supply Service to ensure the highest possible availability of the anti-tank armament.
As of June 1991, it will be possible to improve the anti-tank rocket launcher to combat reactive armour.
The improved HEAT munition for the anti-tank rocket launcher will reach acquisition maturity during the 1991-1995 implementation phase.
Dynamit Nobel SA will deliver the material needed for training instructors in the second quarter of 1992.
The main part of the deliveries of anti-tank rocket launchers will take place from late 1992 until mid-1995.
As of June 1991, the domestic share of orders in the anti-tank rocket launcher programme will be about 101 million Swiss francs, or 35%.
As of June 1991, partial licensed production of the anti-tank rocket launcher in Switzerland will cost 25.6 million Swiss francs more than direct manufacture, or 9.8% more.
As of June 1991, the investments at the Federal Munitions Factory in Thun will create the conditions for later manufacture of improved HEAT munitions and additional practice cartridges.
As of June 1991, once the anti-tank rocket launcher is introduced, subsequent costs will be lower than those currently arising from the use of the 8.3 cm rocket tube.
As of June 1991, after the anti-tank rocket launcher is introduced, no additional storage space and no additional maintenance measures will be required.
As of June 1991, the HPz G 86 warhead of the Dragon anti-tank guided missile will be fitted with a small precursor charge intended to detonate explosive reactive armour and clear the path for the main charge.
Under the 1991 armament programme, about one third of the existing Dragon guided missiles, namely those that did not undergo the first conversion, will be converted.
As of June 1991, the upgraded Dragon guided missile will be introduced into the troops during normal service periods without problems.
As of June 1991, the three fuse elements of the Dragon Tandem warhead will be replaced by an integrated system that still has to be developed.
Delivery of Dragon guided missiles equipped with the Tandem HPz G 90 warhead will begin in 1993 and continue until mid-1997.
As of June 1991, the proposed Dragon improvement will not require any subsequent costs and no additional storage space.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, delivery of the 1,530 laser rangefinders will take place between 1994 and 1997.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, deliveries of anti-personnel mines, fragmentation mines and explosive charges will take place from 1993 to early 1995.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, two additional jobs will be required at the War Material Supply Office for maintenance.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, the annual cost of maintenance equipment will be about 300,000 Swiss francs.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, no additional premises will be needed for the storage and maintenance of the laser rangefinders.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, training facilities for the explosive cratering charges will need to be created at existing training grounds, at a cost of about 250,000 Swiss francs.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, each mortar section of the heavy fusilier companies, airport mortar companies and cyclist mortar companies will receive three laser rangefinders for its observers.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, each command section and each anti-tank section will also receive one laser rangefinder.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, each of the 195 non-upgraded Panzer 68 tanks will be equipped with the same laser rangefinder.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, the acquisition of laser rangefinders will benefit the Swiss job market by 100 percent.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, Leica Heerbrugg SA will manufacture the laser rangefinders under licence from Simrad Optronics A/S of Oslo, Norway.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, the batteries and chargers will again be manufactured by Leclanché SA of Yverdon.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, additional maintenance equipment will be purchased directly from Simrad A/S in Oslo.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, the current maintenance and testing means will need to be increased.
Under the proposed 1991 Swiss armament programme, the FARGOF fire-control system will be assigned to the fire-direction batteries of the 12 cm fortress mortars, the 10.5 cm and 15 cm guns that will remain temporarily in Army 95, and later to the Bison installations.
The zero series of the FARGOF fire-control system for Swiss fortress artillery will be developed in 1994.
From 1994, it will be possible to deliver fifteen FARGOF fire-control systems to Swiss troops each month.
Delivery of all FARGOF fire-control system material to the Swiss Army will be completed around the end of 1996.
As of June 1991, a FARGOF instruction centre will be created at the military training areas of Saint-Maurice, Mels and Airolo in Switzerland.
As of June 1991, the total cost of the FARGOF instruction centre at Saint-Maurice, Mels and Airolo will be about 1 million Swiss francs, to be requested in a future construction budget.
As of June 1991, additional high- and low-current installations required to integrate FARGOF into fortress artillery pieces will cost about 7 million Swiss francs and will be included in one of the next construction programmes under terrain reinforcement.
As of June 1991, Switzerland will acquire AMZ 91 proximity fuses for its 15.5 cm armoured howitzers.
As of June 1991, the AMZ 91 proximity fuses will be assigned to the basic and complementary ammunition lots of the Swiss artillery and will replace the current MZZ 68 impact fuses.
Deliveries of AMZ 91 proximity fuses for 15.5 cm armoured howitzers to the Swiss Army will take place from 1993 to the end of 1995.
As of June 1991, about 70% of the AMZ 91 proximity fuse order will be awarded to Swiss industry.
As of June 1991, the AMZ 91 proximity fuse order will provide work for about 50 people for three years.
As of June 1991, the second series of 15.5 cm CARGO munitions will be assigned to the basic and complementary stocks and to the reserve of the Swiss artillery; the steel and smoke shells replaced by CARGO shells will be converted into practice munitions for schools and courses.
About 200 to 300 CARGO shells will be fired at a foreign firing range over about 25 years after their delivery to the Swiss Army between 1993 and 1995.
CARGO shells delivered between 1993 and 1995 will be inspected for the first time 10 years after delivery and thereafter about every five years.
Deliveries of the second series of 15.5 cm CARGO munitions to the Swiss Army will take place between 1993 and 1995.
As of June 1991, the Swiss industry share of the second-series 15.5 cm CARGO munitions order will be 15%.
As of June 1991, Israel Military Industries (IMI) will offset about 85% of its second-series 15.5 cm CARGO munitions order through purchases made in Switzerland.
As of June 1991, in the future, the artillery of Swiss Mountain Corps 3 will consist of Bison fortress artillery pieces and towed 46 howitzers.
As of June 1991, the 10.5 cm cannon 35 will no longer be used by the Swiss Army after the implementation of Army 95 in 1995.
As of June 1991, the Bison fortress artillery system will be deployed on major communication axes, while the improved 10.5 cm howitzers will be used in intermediate terrain.
As of June 1991, the 10.5 cm howitzers in the Swiss field army corps will be phased out under Army 95 in 1995.
As of June 1991, the proposed range increase for 10.5 cm howitzers will apply only to those of Swiss Mountain Corps 3.
As of June 1991, the artillery regiments of the Swiss mountain divisions will be reorganised into three identical howitzer groups, each equipped with 18 improved-range 10.5 cm howitzer 46.
By 1995, the modernised 10.5 cm howitzer 46 will fire its 10.5 cm steel shell to a range of 13.9 km, up from 10.8 km.
The range-improvement programme for the 10.5 cm howitzer 46 will run from 1992 to 1995.
As of June 1991, a 10.5 cm proximity fuze for the 10.5 cm howitzer 46 will be proposed in a future Swiss armament programme.
The 108 35 mm anti-aircraft guns 63/75 upgraded under the 1989 Swiss armament programme could remain in service beyond 2010.
Under the Armée 95 planning for 1995, all medium-calibre anti-aircraft units of the Swiss anti-aircraft regiments will be equipped with guns upgraded to improved combat value.
Under the 1991 Swiss armament programme, 80 additional 35 mm anti-aircraft guns 63/75 will be refurbished, allowing the remaining six medium anti-aircraft groups of the 15 in Anti-Aircraft Brigade 33 to be equipped between 1994 and 1996.
The 35 mm anti-aircraft guns assigned to medium anti-aircraft batteries at Swiss airfields will not be upgraded under the 1991 armament programme.
The 35 mm anti-aircraft guns assigned to medium anti-aircraft batteries at Swiss airfields will probably be scrapped during the 1990s.
The complete replacement of the Swiss medium-calibre anti-aircraft gun system with a more modern system will not take place during the 1990s.
Switzerland will retain 188 of the 264 originally acquired medium-calibre anti-aircraft guns and extend their service life through upgrades until around 2010.
The 75 Skyguard fire-control units will undergo a further upgrade so that they can remain in service at least beyond 2010.
The 35 mm anti-aircraft guns that are not upgraded will probably be scrapped for technical reasons during the 1990s.
As of June 1991, twenty additional frequency converters will be acquired for Swiss anti-aircraft training to replace generators in buildings and on training areas and firing ranges.
Sixteen additional basic simulators will be acquired under the 1991 Swiss armament programme and delivered between the second half of 1993 and the end of 1994, covering all Swiss anti-aircraft training needs.
The first 16 basic simulators already ordered for Swiss anti-aircraft training will be delivered from mid-1992.
The annual inflation rate applied to the 35 mm anti-aircraft gun upgrade will be 4% from 1991 to the end of 1994.

Under the 1991 Swiss armament programme, four new PC-9 aircraft will be acquired and used for target towing in firing exercises, replacing the C-3605 aircraft.
From 1991, Swiss PC-7 aircraft, helicopters and Hunter combat aircraft will be used temporarily for target spotting.
The acquisition of the remaining eight PC-9 aircraft will be deferred to the 1992-1995 implementation phase.
Under the 1991 Swiss armament programme, the four PC-9 aircraft will be operated by the target-spotting squadron of Swiss Aviation Brigade 31 exclusively for anti-aircraft troops.
As of June 1991, if simulators for anti-aircraft systems currently under development can be acquired soon, the Swiss Army will need about 16 aircraft for target simulation, with eight equipped for target towing and eight used for target spotting.

As of June 1991, the proposed acquisition of the PC-9 aircraft and RM-24 target-towing systems will be managed by the line organisation of the Swiss Armament Group.
About 40 Swiss suppliers will participate in the acquisition of the PC-9 aircraft and RM-24 target-towing systems through subcontracts by the end of 1992.
Once the four PC-9 aircraft are in service after their delivery in the fourth quarter of 1992, the preparation and maintenance work per flight hour will be half that of the Vampire aircraft previously used by the Swiss military.
As of June 1991, the fuel consumption per flight hour of the PC-9 will be 80% lower than that of the jet-powered Vampire aircraft previously used by the Swiss military.
As of June 1991, the acquisition and operation of the four PC-9 aircraft will require no expenditure for additional personnel or buildings.
As of June 1991, spare parts that will have to be acquired later for the PC-9 fleet will cause higher costs than if more spare parts were purchased with the initial acquisition.

As of September 1965, the GATT Part IV protocol will become legally valid for Switzerland, a provisional GATT member, only after Switzerland has signed it and two thirds of GATT full members have accepted it.
The GATT Part IV protocol will remain open for acceptance until 31 December 1965.
The Swiss Federal Assembly will deal conclusively with the approval of the GATT Part IV protocol in its December 1965 session.
As of September 1965, the GATT Part IV protocol will also apply to the Principality of Liechtenstein as long as Liechtenstein remains connected to Switzerland by a customs union.
Switzerland's declaration on its provisional accession to GATT will remain valid until 31 December 1967.
As of September 1965, the new GATT Part IV provisions will not affect the Swiss Agricultural Act or the other Swiss legislation protecting agriculture.
